**Who resamples into the live rate (V3).** `Reopening` and `ScoStuck` open the output at the rate the device reports *now* — 16000 through the SCO tail, because waiting for 44100 costs 2591 ms to the first render callback against 48 ms for opening eagerly — while every lane ring is 44 100 by definition (`AudioFormat::lane()`), `deckd-tts`'s resampler always targets the lane rate and the chimes are pre-rendered at it. The bridge is the **mixer**, inside the render callback: it pops lane frames at `44100/out_rate` per output frame and interpolates, so a 10 ms cut is still 10 ms of lane audio whatever rate the stream is open at. It is *not* cpal's job — passing `LANE_RATE` to `build_output_stream` makes cpal call `set_physical_format`/`set_sample_rate` and fight the HFP link, which a Bluetooth endpoint cannot honour. Without that bridge every post-PTT announcement played at 0.363× speed and about sixteen semitones flat for the whole 2.3 s resume, and the whole of `ScoStuck` did.

The output stream stays open while the route is A2DP (fixes the first-chime clipping of a lazily opened stream); it is closed only for our own capture or when the device flips the rate under us. **Closing it during our own capture is load-bearing, not precautionary** (V5, measured 2026-09-21, answering `host-research/voice-macos.md:187`): on the SCO link the deck's speaker returns into the deck's own microphone at ≈**+17 dB** — a −26 dBFS tone came back at −11.9 dBFS RMS — and there is no acoustic echo cancellation anywhere in this path. Anything we play while the mic is open is fed straight back into STT. The same measurement is why `[voice] speak_over_sco` (§7.5) must **never** play while a capture is live: it is for the post-release reply, not for talking over the operator. (The loopback saturated, so treat +17 dB as an order of magnitude rather than a figure.)

cpal reports the rate flip as `StreamInvalidated` and a BT drop as `DeviceNotAvailable` (`host-research/voice-macos.md:42`). If the output UID is absent: `[voice] fallback_output = "none"` (default: silent; GUI/tray show "deck audio not connected") or `"system_default"` for solo users.

Closing and reopening the output is a **cpal contract, not a hardware requirement** (V4): an output AUHAL held open across both rate flips produced no CoreAudio error at all — 51–59 render callbacks while the nominal rate was 16000, continuing after the flip back. The nominal-rate listener is accurate to ~30–50 ms, so the route SM can trust its events instead of polling. cpal reports the rate flip as `StreamInvalidated` and a BT drop as `DeviceNotAvailable` (`host-research/voice-macos.md:42`), and two things this document never said are true of both (V19): cpal **pauses the unit before** calling the error callback, so the route SM must not assume the stream is still running when it hears about it; and **the error callback is real-time code** — cpal's `kAudioDeviceProcessorOverload` (`Xrun`) listener fires on the render thread and calls the user callback there — so it may not allocate, lock or log. It raises a flag with one atomic `fetch_or` and `deckd-audio` drains it on its tick.

**Stream lifetime.** The route SM above closes and reopens the output stream on every PTT and every rate flip, and the lane rings have to survive that. **Not** because the stream cannot move between threads: `assert_stream_send!(Stream)` and `assert_stream_sync!(Stream)` are asserted inside cpal 0.18.2's own coreaudio backend (`host/coreaudio/mod.rs:142`) and a probe compiled them green, so the reason this document used to give — "cpal's coreaudio `Stream` is `!Send`" — is **false for this version** (V10, 2026-09-21), and the "built, kept and dropped on `deckd-audio` only" constraint goes with it. The stream lives on `deckd-audio` by the `deckd-hid-probe` precedent (a permanent thread owning a run-loop resource), not by a type bound. The real reason for the hand-off is one line of `DeviceTrait::build_*_stream`: `D: FnMut(..) + Send + 'static`. The consumers live **inside** that closure and the closure dies with the stream, so a closure that owned its `rtrb::Consumer` halves outright would destroy them while `deckd-tts` still held the matching producers — the next stream would have nothing to read, and the Question the operator has not heard yet would be gone, once per press. So the lanes live in an `Arc<Lanes>` where each lane is `{ producer_side: Mutex<Option<Producer>> (taken once by deckd-tts), consumer: Mutex<Option<Consumer>>, state: AtomicU8, gain: AtomicU32 }`: a new render closure **takes** every `Consumer` out of its `Mutex` at build time (on `deckd-audio`, before the stream starts — never in the callback) and a small `Drop` guard inside the closure **puts them back** when the stream is dropped, so the same rings pass from stream to stream and queued Question/Ready audio survives a PTT hold. Held items are simply not consumed while no stream is open. `deckd-audio` is a **pure control loop** (`crossbeam::select` over route commands, capture commands and a 50 ms tick): it never synthesises or resamples, so `Capture::Open` — the latency-critical first step of a PTT — can never queue behind a synth; synthesis and resampling happen on `deckd-tts`, fed by the AVSpeech buffer callbacks that only the main thread can receive (§7.6, V13), and only ever touch the producer halves.

**On the BLE transport** the same machine runs with its settled rate moved from 44 100 to the deck speaker's 16 000 (`RouteMachine::set_transport`), fed by `deckd-ble-link` instead of the CoreAudio property watcher: the link becoming ready is `NominalRate{16000}` and both sides present (as far as the deck's info characteristic offers them), anything else is both sides gone. What §7.2 is for still holds — a capture of ours closes the output (the deck discards speaker frames while its microphone streams anyway, and V5's +17 dB of loopback is the same speaker and the same microphone), the release reopens it — but there is nothing else to fold: no SCO tail (the release reopens and settles in the same turn), no `ScoExternal` (no other app can open this microphone), nothing to get stuck on, and `speak_over_sco` has no effect. The two states that name the transport are spelled for it: a ready BLE speaker is `ble` (not `a2dp`) and our own capture `ble_ours` (not `sco_ours`); `None`, `Reopening` (for the moment between the release and the reopen), `ScoStuck` (two failed opens, retried when the link comes back) and `SystemDefault` keep their names. The engine reports which transport it was built for once, as `VoiceIn::Transport`: the snapshot carries it as `voice.transport` (`"ble"` or `"coreaudio"`, null with no engine) and every `voice.route` event after it carries `transport`, which is how the GUI draws the BLE transport's own diagram. `output_uid`/`input_uid` are `ble:<CoreBluetooth identifier>` (§7.9), or `usb:<serial number>` when the deck's cable is the port in use: the one link handle has two ports (`ble::Transport`), CoreBluetooth's and the hid thread's (report channel 7, `ble::UsbPort`), and shows the cable whenever it is ready, with Bluetooth standing in otherwise.

### 7.3 Mixer (RT-safe, in the render callback)

Lanes, highest first: `Ptt` (virtual: while a PTT session is live everything is cut), `Chime`, `Question`, `Ready`, `Chatter`. Each lane is an SPSC ring of interleaved stereo f32 at 44.1 kHz (2 s) filled by the `deckd-tts` thread through the producer halves and drained by whichever render closure currently holds the consumer halves (§7.2); the render callback sums lanes × per-lane gain × master gain (`set speaker` 0..10 on a perceptual curve, 0 = mute), applies a 10 ms cut fade and a 120 ms duck, touches only atomics (`AtomicU8` lane state, `AtomicU32` gain targets), allocates nothing and takes no lock. Pre-emption: a higher-priority utterance ducks lower lanes to −18 dB for `Chime` (≤400 ms) and cuts `Chatter` for `Question`/`Ready`; `Question` and `Ready` queue FIFO with TTL 60 s; `Chatter` TTL 20 s, superseded by a newer reply from the same agent. Chimes are pre-rendered PCM at 44.1 k and 16 k (question: two-tone rising; ready: single soft knock, `host-research/spec.md:114-115`). Across a PTT hold, `Question`/`Ready` items are **held, not flushed**, except items about the agent the operator just addressed (marked stale by the dispatch). A **new** render closure starts every lane's cut envelope at **0.0**, not at unity: §7.2 rebuilds the stream on every press and every rate flip, so "the first quantum of a fresh mixer" is what the operator hears after every release, and there is no signal in flight to fade down from — the previous closure's envelopes died with it and the device was not being fed at all in between. Starting at unity faded every held lane down from full level and drained it on the way (measured: a 1.0494 peak, over full scale, with a Question and a Ready queued, and 880 frames of the held Ready consumed). From silence the lane that plays fades in over the same 10 ms, which is also the click a stream start otherwise makes. A flush is resolved **where it is raised**: the control loop empties the ring itself whenever no render closure holds the consumer (a `voice.stop` during `ScoOurs`, a TTL expiring mid-hold, the release's `Chatter` flush), and a closure that is dropped owing a flush honours it on the way out. The flag may only ever discard audio that was queued when it was raised — carrying it across a stream rebuild applied it to the *next* item on the lane instead, which cost a held Question or not depending on a race with the 11.61 ms callback period.

**What the fade can and cannot do** (V6, measured 2026-09-21). Round-trip audio latency on this link is ≈**470 ms**: five 300 ms bursts came back with an onset delay of 460–480 ms and an offset delay of 480–500 ms, the burst duration preserved — a pure delay line, not a smear. CoreAudio's own latency properties under-report that by ≈400 ms: `kAudioDevicePropertyLatency + SafetyOffset + BufferFrameSize` adds up to **30.0 ms** during SCO. So the 10 ms cut fade silences the **mixer** well inside its budget — §15's row 4 measures −60 dB within 35 ms, and that number stays, because it is what we control and it is genuinely measured — while the operator still hears roughly **half a second of the previous utterance** coming down the Bluetooth pipe after `ptt act:1`. That tail is the pipe, not a fade to be tuned; the press chime has to cover it, and **a fade must never be sized from the CoreAudio latency properties**, which would suggest 30 ms and be wrong by more than an order of magnitude. The callback's own budget is 512 frames / **11.61 ms** on a CoreAudio time-constraint (real-time) thread (`period = computation = constraint = 11.610 ms`, inter-callback 11.44–11.65 ms, V18), so a 10 ms fade spans one callback and the 35 ms budget spans three.

### 7.4 PTT session state machine (core)

```
 Idle ──ptt act:1 {agent,ctx,card?} / voice.listen─────────▶ Arming{target,ctx,card,t0, principal, session}
   │                                                          │ mixer.cut_all(); capture.open(input UID | fallback); stt.start(locale, hints)
   │                                                          ▼
   │                                                        Listening ── partials → outbox Transcript (keep-latest, ≤4/s, last 120 B) + transcript.partial
   │                                                          ├─ act:0 abort:0 ─▶ Finalizing: capture.close(); stt.finish() (complete final only; failure aborts)
   │                                                          │        → Transcript{final:1} → Dispatch(ctx) → Restoring → Idle
   │                                                          ├─ act:0 abort:1 | CANCEL | up | link drop | 60 s ─▶ Aborting: capture.close(); stt.cancel(); voice.state{state:"aborted", reason} → Restoring → Idle
   │                                                          └─ `channel` arriving mid-session is applied after release (G:222-223)
   Arming: 2.5 s without a NON-ZERO sample (V2; buffers start at ~28 ms full of digital silence) → voice.state{error:"mic unavailable"}, transcript "(no mic)", perm.changed if TCC denied
```

**Release waits for the complete transcript.** Partials are display previews only.
The old 1.5 s reducer deadline could dispatch a partial while Whisper was still
finishing; both that fallback and the audio engine's error fallback are removed.
The audio engine waits up to 30 s by default. Whisper allows 10 s plus the audio
duration, bounded to 30–90 s, measured after the capture tail is drained; a
successful decode returns immediately. A 120 s reducer watchdog covers a hung
engine. The audio loop polls final results in release order without blocking
capture or controls for the next press. The device retains its transcript with
a TRANSCRIBING indicator; a device-local press identity is echoed on both
results and failures so an old result cannot dismiss a newer view. Completed
transcript controls survive previews from later presses in the outbox.
At most four final decodes can be
pending; further presses report `audio_busy` until a slot becomes free.
Timeout or decode failure emits `voice.state{state:"aborted", error}` and
sends no order or answer. Final results and final failures consume the same
ordered session slot, so a late result cannot be sent as the next PTT press.

**Corrected by measurement (V2, 2026-09-21).** "1.5 s without samples" would never fire on a dead microphone: the deck's input callback starts at ~28 ms, `AudioUnitRender` returns `noErr`, and **every sample is an exact digital zero until 1031 ms** (median of 16 trials, 815–1246 ms). Samples arrive on a microphone that is not there. So `Arming → Listening` is keyed on the **first sample that is not an exact digital zero**, the deadline is `ARMING_LIVENESS = 2500 ms` (measured worst case plus margin), and the edge has a real producer: `deckd-audio`'s capture callback raises one bit per buffer (`deckd::audio::buffer_is_live`) and the control loop turns the first one into `VoiceIn::Live` for the reducer. It is **not** derived from the RMS meter — the meter is scaled by `set mic`, so `set mic 0` reads digital silence on a microphone that is working perfectly, and keying liveness off it would declare it dead.

The session records **who started it**: `principal:"device"` for a deck `ptt` edge, else the caller of `voice.listen` with its session id; every `op.answer`/`op.order` the dispatch issues carries that principal, so the trust checks of §4.2 apply exactly as if the caller had typed the answer. For `ctx:"answer"` on a permission/elicitation card the guard runs in `Idle`, before `Arming`: an untrusted caller gets `-32002` and no capture is opened. Dispatch by `ctx` (P:134, G:208-228): `talk` → grammar (`report|status`, `stop|quiet`, §5.6) else `op.order{source:"ptt"}`; empty final → nothing dispatched, and the session ends on **`voice.state{state:"ended", empty:true}`**. `answer` on a type `q` card → `op.answer{text}` after the intent grammar on permission cards → owner delivery → `card clear` on success (same 4 s budget); on a type `r` card (the device latches the Ready id, `agents.rs:165-169`) → `op.order{source:"ptt"}` to the owner, then `card clear`. Unlike a chosen answer, a PTT `ctx:answer` release does not lock the device card as "sent" (only `answer()` sets `sent_at_ms`, `agents.rs:207-221`), so a delivery failure shows nothing on the deck: on `failed` after a PTT answer the daemon speaks "not delivered to <name>" on the `Question` lane, badges the GUI card, and leaves the card pending on the device (no clear). `name` → `device.name{text}` cut to 16 B on a char boundary (5 CJK chars, `host-research/critic.md:15`), title-cased for Latin scripts. `codex` → never captured; only the cut. `Restoring` = the route SM's `Reopening`.

**The end of a session is `voice.state`, not a `ptt.ended` event kind.** This section named that edge `ptt.ended` before §4.12 had a row for it, and no `EventKind` was ever added; the decision is that there is no new kind. §4.12 already carries `voice.state`, and a second kind for the same edge would have to be subscribed to separately — every client already watching `voice.*` would miss exactly the event that says a session is over. So every release emits `voice.state{state:"ended", empty:bool}` beside the session's other fields; `empty` is the fact nothing else gives, because "nothing was dispatched" and "the transcript was empty" look identical from outside (no `op.order`, no `op.answer`, no `card.answered`) and are completely different things — the first is worth showing the operator, the second is them pressing the button and saying nothing. An abort ends on `voice.state{state:"aborted"}` and never reaches `ended`.

**The `Arming` liveness deadline counts non-zero samples, not buffers, and it is 2.5 s** (V2, measured 2026-09-21). CoreAudio starts the input callback at ~28 ms and `AudioUnitRender` returns `noErr` for about a second of exact digital zeros — on a live microphone and on a dead one alike — so the design's "1.5 s without samples" would never have fired on a genuinely dead mic. The deadline is twice the measured worst-case bring-up (1246 ms) because the expensive failure is the other direction: a working microphone declared dead aborts the capture and throws the operator's sentence away, while reporting a dead one a second later costs nothing. It also has to stay above `[voice] slow_sco_ms` (§7.5), or a session would die before it could file its own "this was slow" report.

### 7.5 Mitigations for the SCO bring-up (measured 2026-09-21)

Bring-up — press report → first **non-zero** mic sample — is **1031 ms median** (815 min, 1246 max, n=16). A2DP resume — close input → output nominal back to 44100 — is **2307 ms median** (2281–2447, n=16) and is a fixed macOS SCO-teardown timer that cannot be hurried. Both of §16 Q2's thresholds are blown (V1), so both of its defaults flip, and the mitigations below are no longer hedges against an unknown.

1. `Capture::Open` is dispatched on the press report before any other work, then the mixer cut; `deckd-audio` is a control loop with nothing else on it (§7.2), so the open is never queued behind a synth.
2. One warm `SFSpeechAudioBufferRecognitionRequest` per session is created ahead of time so the recogniser is ready when the first buffer lands.
3. `mic_warm = "card_pending"` (§7.1) if measurement shows > 300 ms bring-up. **Measured: 1031 ms median (815–1246, n=16) to the first non-zero sample, so this is the shipped default.** It is implemented in `deckd-audio`, which is told by the reducer whether a Question card is pending (`AudioCmd::Warm`) and decides for itself what to do about it; a press then **inherits** that already-open stream rather than opening a second one, which is the whole point. Two rules keep it from costing more than it saves: speaking beats warming (a warm input holds the link in SCO with the output closed, so an announcement made over it is one nobody hears — `pump` gives the microphone back before it starts one), and a warm-up waits for the lanes to go quiet first, so it never silences the very question that tells the operator to press.
4. First partial later than 800 ms after the press → `voice.route{slow_sco_ms}` so the GUI can surface it; `metrics.get.sco` keeps the last 20 timings. **Corrected (V2):** keyed off the first **non-zero sample**, not the first partial or the first callback, and compared against `[voice] slow_sco_ms` (default 1500 ms, above the measured worst case of 1246 ms so it does not fire on every press). It is an exception report: `voice.route` carries `slow_sco_ms` only when the threshold was passed, and only once per press.
5. `Reopening` opens the output at the device's **current** nominal rate at once and resamples into it, rather than waiting for 44100 (V3): waiting costs 2591 ms to the first render callback, opening eagerly at whatever the device reports (16000 during the SCO tail) costs 48 ms — a 54× difference on every post-PTT announcement.
6. First **non-zero sample** later than `[voice] slow_sco_ms = 1500` after the press → `voice.route{slow_sco_ms}` so the GUI can surface it; `metrics.get.sco` keeps the last 20 timings. The design's "first partial later than 800 ms" would have fired on every press (V2): the threshold has to sit above the measured bring-up and below §7.4's 2.5 s `Arming` deadline.

None of the above applies to the BLE transport (§7.9): the deck streams from the press itself, so there is no bring-up to hide and no resume to wait out. `slow_sco_ms` still reports a press whose first non-zero sample came late, which on BLE means the deck's START itself was late.

### 7.6 STT and TTS engines

```rust
type BoxFuture<'a, T> = Pin<Box<dyn Future<Output = T> + Send + 'a>>;   // the lifetime the design omitted (E0106)

trait CaptureSource: Send {                    // V17: §14 assumes this seam, §7 never declared it
    fn open(&mut self, req: &CaptureOpen, sink: CaptureSink) -> Result<CaptureOpened, AudioError>;
    fn close(&mut self); fn is_open(&self) -> bool; }
// CaptureSink = an `FnMut(u32, &[f32]) + Send` frame sink + the RT-safe fault flag (V19)

trait SttEngine: Send + Sync { fn info(&self) -> EngineInfo;
    fn on_device(&self, locale: &str) -> OnDevice;                      // answers -32009 BEFORE anything opens
    fn start(&self, req: &SttStart) -> Result<Box<dyn SttSession>, AudioError>; }
trait SttSession: Send {                       // V13/V16: a Send *handle* that posts to the main thread
    fn feed(&mut self, mono_f32: &[f32], rate: u32);
    fn events(&self) -> Receiver<SttEvent>;                             // Partial | Final | Error{code,message}
    fn finish(self: Box<Self>) -> BoxFuture<'static, Result<String, AudioError>>;
    fn cancel(self: Box<Self>); }

trait TtsEngine: Send + Sync { fn info(&self) -> EngineInfo; fn voices(&self) -> Vec<VoiceInfo>;
    fn synthesize(&self, req: &TtsRequest) -> Result<Box<dyn TtsJob>, AudioError>; }
trait TtsJob: Send { fn format(&self) -> AudioFormat;                   // known before the first chunk is read
    fn chunks(&self) -> Receiver<TtsChunk>;
    fn stopper(&self) -> Arc<dyn TtsStop>; }                            // barge-in, from another thread
trait TtsStop: Send + Sync { fn stop(&self); }

enum RouteEvent { NominalRate{rate}, DevicePresent{side, present}, ExternalCapture{running},   // V17
                  OurCapture{open, rate}, OutputOpened{rate}, OutputFailed, Fault(Fault), Tick }
```

**Corrected against a compiled probe (2026-09-21; V13, V16, V17, V19).** What this document first published here was four lines of pseudo-Rust that do not compile and could not be implemented against Apple's frameworks if they did. `BoxFuture`/`BoxStream` named no lifetime (`E0106`). `Box<dyn SttSession>` was `!Send` although it is created on the core and fed from the capture thread — and `Send` alone is not enough: `SFSpeechRecognizer`'s result handlers and `AVSpeechSynthesizer`'s buffer callbacks are delivered on the **main thread only** (measured: a worker thread with no run loop, 0 buffers; a worker driving its own run loop, 0 buffers; the main thread driving its run loop, 300/300), so the session is a `Send` handle that posts work to main (§2.3's `CFRunLoop`) and reads results back through a channel. `partials() -> watch::Receiver<String>` could not carry an error, and errors arrive **mid-session** — `kAFAssistantErrorDomain 1110` was observed while the button was still held — which is exactly what §7.4's dead-mic path needs to hear; partials, an early `isFinal` and errors now share one ordered channel. `TtsEngine::synthesize` discarded the audio format although AVSpeech's rate varies per voice (measured 22050/1/f32 for Samantha) and the resampler has to exist before the PCM does, so the format is known before the first chunk. There was no TTS `stop()` at all, and barge-in runs on a different thread from the one draining the job, so it is a separate `Send + Sync` handle rather than `&mut self`. And `CaptureSource` and `RouteEvent` did not exist: without them the cpal → `rtrb` → `AVAudioPCMBuffer` path, the mic gain, the RMS meter and the whole route machine have no test that does not need a microphone, a Bluetooth link and a TCC grant.

v1 STT `apple`: `SFSpeechRecognizer(locale)` via objc2-speech 0.3.2, `requiresOnDeviceRecognition` when `supportsOnDeviceRecognition` (en-US on-device verified on this Mac, `host-research/voice-macos.md:56`), `shouldReportPartialResults = true`, `SttStart.contextual` = agent names + the pending card's choice labels (`contextualStrings`) so "cohdl-api" survives recognition; the 60 s PTT cap matches Apple's 1-minute server limit. Locale in `[voice.stt] locale` (the Settings face has no language row, `host-research/critic.md:18`); a locale without on-device support (zh-CN here) is refused with `-32009` unless `[voice.stt] allow_server = true` (audio leaves the Mac; the GUI says so). Engines behind the trait for v1.1/v2: `openai-realtime` (24 kHz PCM via rubato), `deepgram` (16 k linear16 native), `SpeechTranscriber` (Swift helper), Parakeet (`fluidaudio-rs`); API keys live in the Keychain item `ai.cohdl.openwalkietalkie`, never in `config.toml`.

STT `whisper` (opt-in since 2026-10-11, `deckd::audio::whisper`, `[voice.stt] engine = "whisper"`): a Whisper model on the Mac — whisper.cpp through `whisper-rs` 0.16 with the Metal backend — behind the same `SttEngine` seam as Apple's, which stays underneath for every press the model cannot take yet. **The weights are not in the build.** They are fetched only when the operator opts in: the app asks once at first start (one dialog for both local models — "Keep Apple's", "Recogniser only", "Recogniser and voice" — recorded in `[voice.stt] prompted` so it is asked once per Mac, never per launch), the Voice screen's **Recognition** control and `deckd stt install` do the same later. The catalogue (`deckd::stt::MODELS`) is `large-v3-turbo` (1.6 GB, the default), its `q8_0` (874 MB) and `q5_0` (574 MB) quantisations, `small` (488 MB) and `base.en` (148 MB); the daemon downloads through `/usr/bin/curl -C -` into `Caches/OpenWalkieTalkie/stt/` (`stt.install{model?}` → background thread, `stt.status` → progress, `stt.remove` → delete; a file is "present" only at exactly the catalogue's size, so a part file never counts) and loads a present model the moment it is wanted — at start, on every reload, and when a `stt.status` read finds it just landed — so the first press after a download does not pay the ~2 s load. Why: Apple's recogniser has no on-device Chinese asset here, so a `zh-CN` press is refused unless speech goes to Apple's server; Whisper recognises Chinese and English in one utterance on the machine, with `[voice.stt] language = "auto"` (the default) detecting per press, an ISO 639-1 code pinning it, and empty following `locale`. The live transcript: Whisper decodes a buffer and does not stream, so the session's thread re-decodes the audio so far every ~0.9 s once ≥0.4 s is new and reports the text as a partial, and the final is one decode of everything at release — with a 30–90 s failure ceiling (`SttSession::finish_budget`, §7.4). A partial decode the release overtook is aborted through whisper.cpp's abort callback so the final never queues behind it; dropping a timed-out final also cancels its decode (2026-10-11, after a report that fast speech released right after the last word lost its tail) (greedy, `no_context`, `suppress_nst`, the contextual strings as the initial prompt, audio under 1.1 s padded with silence, the Mac's fallback microphone resampled to 16 kHz by linear interpolation). `voice.engines` names `whisper` only while it is selected **and** the model is on disk — a selected engine with no weights is `apple` — and every `[voice.stt]` key is hot (the engine reads a cell the core refreshes). Recognition by default is unchanged: Apple's on-device recogniser, and nothing leaves the machine with either engine.

TTS `kokoro` (opt-in since 2026-10-11, `deckd::audio::kokoro`, `[voice.tts] engine = "kokoro"`): a Kokoro v1.1 voice on the Mac — the Chinese-and-English model through the official `sherpa-onnx` crate (1.13.8, a prebuilt static library fetched once at build time; `SHERPA_ONNX_ARCHIVE_DIR` points it at a copy), which carries the text front ends (a Chinese lexicon, a jieba dictionary and number/date/phone rules, espeak data for English) — behind the same `TtsEngine` seam, with Apple's synthesiser underneath for every utterance it cannot take yet. **The package is not in the build**: it is fetched only on the opt-in — the app's first-start prompt ("Recogniser and voice"), the Voice screen's Spoken replies control, `deckd tts install` — through the same downloader as the recogniser (`deckd::download`: curl with resume, a `.tar.bz2` unpacked beside the target and moved into place, present once `model.onnx`, `voices.bin` and `tokens.txt` are there) into `Caches/OpenWalkieTalkie/tts/kokoro/<package>/`; the catalogue (`deckd::kokoro::PACKAGES`) is `kokoro-multi-lang-v1_1` (365 MB, the default) and its int8 twin (147 MB); `tts.install{model?}`, `tts.status`, `tts.remove` mirror the `stt.*` methods. The engine loads the package when it is wanted and on disk (at start, on each reload, when a `tts.status` read finds it just landed) and speaks sentence by sentence: sherpa-onnx calls back with each sentence's samples (`max_num_sentences = 1`), each goes to `deckd-tts` as a chunk, barge-in returns `false` from the callback and ends the generation (measured on the M5: first sentence at ~0.33 s, 2.7 s of English in 0.7 s, 4.4 s of Chinese in 1.0 s, on the CPU). The 103 speakers are addressed by name or id (`deckd::kokoro::SPEAKERS`: `af_maple`, `af_sol`, `bf_vale`, then the Chinese `zf_*`/`zm_*` voices in the order sherpa-onnx's conversion wrote them); the agents' pool is `[voice.tts] voices`, else `af_maple`, `zm_010`, `bf_vale`, `zf_001`; a name the package does not have is mapped onto the pool by the same stable hash as the other engines. In the engine chain the local voice sits under the cloud engine (and under the sidecar), so `engine = "cloud"` with no key, `"kokoro"` with no package, and `"apple"` all end at Apple's, and `voice.engines` names `kokoro` only while it is selected and its package is on disk. `deckd tts install --mlx` keeps the Qwen3-TTS sidecar path.

TTS `cloud` (the default since 2026-10-11, `deckd::audio::cloud`) — **bring your own key.** With `[voice.tts] api_key` set, the announcements are synthesised by `provider`'s hosted model: OpenAI's `/v1/audio/speech` (`gpt-4o-mini-tts` by default; `instructions` carries `[voice.tts] instruct`) or ElevenLabs' `/v1/text-to-speech/{voice}/stream` (`eleven_flash_v2_5`; voice names resolved to ids through the account's `/v2/voices` once per key). Without a key — the state every install starts in — Apple's synthesiser speaks, on the machine, and nothing leaves it. The engine is built over Apple's at start and **reads `[voice.tts]` at every utterance** from a cell the core refreshes on each reload (`Core::tts_settings`), so a key pasted into `config.toml` or written by `deckd tts key set` (typed without echo, or piped; it edits the file as the operator, through `config::edit`) speaks at the next announcement; `engine`, `provider`, `api_key`, `model`, `voices`, `lang` and `instruct` are all hot for it, and `voice.engines` names the synthesiser in force from the config (`openai`/`elevenlabs`, `on_device: false`; `apple` otherwise) rather than from a cached read. The request goes through `/usr/bin/curl` like the usage probe's: the key rides in a config file on curl's stdin, never on its command line; the headers are asked for on stdout ahead of the body (`--include`) so the status is known before any audio is read; both providers stream raw 24 kHz 16-bit mono PCM, handed to `deckd-tts` chunk by chunk as it arrives, and barge-in kills the transfer. A refusal (4xx/5xx, a network failure, a stalled start) goes to Apple's engine and cools the cloud down for 60 s so every announcement does not pay the round trip to be refused again; a changed key ends the cooldown at once. The agents' voice pool is the engine's own (`[voice.tts] voices`, else OpenAI's `coral`/`ash`/`sage`/`echo` or ElevenLabs' `Sarah`/`George`/`Laura`/`Brian`); a `[voice.voices] default_pool` name the provider does not have is mapped onto it by a stable hash, an OpenAI voice name or an ElevenLabs name/id is used as such. `config.get` publishes `tts.api_key` as a hint (length and last four) and never the key; `Tts`'s `Debug` is the same hint, so a logged config cannot leak it; and the key is a managed key of `config.set` only because the Voice screen's **Spoken replies** control says, where it is entered, that the announcements then leave the machine (the same rule that still holds `voice.stt.allow_server` back). `deckd tts status` reports the engine in force, the provider, the model, the pool and the key hint. Recognition is unchanged: Apple's on-device recogniser, by the operator's decision (no cloud ASR).

TTS `mlx` (`[voice.tts] engine = "mlx"`, `deckd::audio::sidecar`; the default from 2026-10-10 to 2026-10-11, now opt-in because a shipped app would have to carry a Python environment): a neural model on the Mac's own GPU through MLX — Qwen3-TTS 1.7B CustomVoice (8-bit) by default, any `mlx-audio` TTS model by `[voice.tts] model` — in a Python sidecar the daemon owns (`host/tts/sidecar.py`, embedded in the binary and written out under `Caches/OpenWalkieTalkie/tts/`). Nothing leaves the machine. `deckd tts install` builds the sidecar's environment with `uv` (Python 3.12, `mlx-audio`) and loads the model once so it is cached; the daemon starts the sidecar at boot, the sidecar warms the model's graphs on a throwaway utterance before it reports `R`eady, and each announcement is one `say` line on its stdin answered by `H`eader / `P`CM / `E`nd frames on its stdout (16-bit mono at the model's rate, 24 kHz for Qwen3-TTS), streamed as the model produces them so playback starts after the first chunk (measured on an M5: 0.2 s to first audio, 0.6× real time, after the warm-up). Barge-in is a `cancel` line. The agents' voice pool is `[voice.tts] voices` (Qwen3-TTS's `Ryan`, `Aiden`, `Vivian`, `Serena` when empty; `Uncle_Fu`, `Dylan`, `Eric` exist too); a `[voice.voices] default_pool` name the model does not have is mapped onto one of them by a stable hash. Apple's engine stays underneath: while the sidecar is loading, not installed or dead, the utterance goes to it instead of being dropped, with the reason in the log, and the sidecar is restarted at the next utterance after a 10 s pause. `voice.engines` reports `mlx` as on-device. The keys the sidecar is started with (`config::SIDECAR_KEYS`) need a restart only while `mlx` is the engine before or after the change — `config::is_hot_change` — because the cloud engine reads the same keys live. v1 TTS `apple`: `AVSpeechSynthesizer.writeUtterance_toBufferCallback` (objc2-avf-audio 0.3.2) → per-buffer format read (commonly 22.05 kHz mono, varies per voice, `host-research/voice-macos.md:62`, measured 22050/1/f32 for Samantha) → rubato 5.0.0 to 44.1 k stereo → lane. The buffer callbacks arrive **on the main thread and nowhere else** (V13), so main receives them and hands the PCM to `deckd-tts`, which owns the resampler: rubato 5.0.0 is a total API break from 0.x (`Fft::new(…, FixedSync::…)`, buffers through the separate `audioadapter` crates, `process_into_buffer` for the allocation-free path), and 22050→44100 mono resolves to `input_frames_next=1024`, `output_frames_next=2048`, `output_delay=256` (V20). Text sanitiser: strip ANSI/markdown/code fences, first sentence, ≤`[voice.tts] announce_max_chars = 120`. `say -a` remains a `--dev` diagnostic only. **The release's tail (2026-10-11):** `deckd-audio` no longer closes the capture on the HID release line alone. The deck's frames encoded before the release are still crossing the radio, and its END follows the last of them, so `Engine::finish` waits for `CaptureSource::ended()` (the BLE feed's bound stream has ENDed; CoreAudio is always done) for up to `TAIL_GRACE` = 300 ms, draining PCM into the recogniser meanwhile, then closes. END normally lands within ~100 ms; without the wait a fast sentence released right after its last word lost that word.

### 7.7 Per-agent voices and announcements

Voice per agent: `[agents."<key>"].voice` pin → `state.json.voices[key]` (auto-assigned round-robin from `[voice.voices] default_pool` = 4 distinct enhanced en-US voices present on the machine, else the kind default) → GUI picker with preview. Siri voices are not available to AVSpeech (`host-research/voice-macos.md:64`).

Announce policy (`set announce`, host-side, default on, G:147): on `state:ready` speak `"<name>: <announce ?? first sentence of reply>"` on `Ready`; on a new Question card play the question chime and, if the device is not on the Agents face **or** `[voice] announce_questions = true`, speak `"<name> needs a decision: <card text ≤80 chars>"` on `Question`; on `state:error` `"<name> failed"`. `set chime` gates the `Chime` lane only. `voice.say` from API clients obeys the same lanes. Barge-in: `ptt act:1` → the `Ptt` lane's cut plus a `voice.stop` of **`chatter` only** before Arming — the silencing is the cut and the closed output (§7.2/§7.3), while the *discarding* follows §7.3's own rule that `Question`/`Ready` are held and stale `Chatter` is not, so the press does not throw away the Question the operator pressed the button to answer; `ptt ctx:codex` → `voice.stop all`, because there is no capture of ours and therefore no cut to do the silencing.

### 7.8 Permissions flow

`perm.request{microphone|speech}` makes the daemon call `AVCaptureDevice.requestAccess(.audio)` / `SFSpeechRecognizer.requestAuthorization` on its main thread; because `deckd` lives in `Contents/MacOS` of the signed bundle the alert names OpenWalkieTalkie and the grant extends to the daemon (`host-research/voice-macos.md:102`). Hedge (product-ops, `host-research/voice-macos.md:108`): if the launchd-spawned prompt does not surface within 5 s on macOS 27, the daemon answers `{status:"prompt_not_shown"}` and the GUI requests the same grant itself — same bundle, same grant, **which holds only because both Mach-Os are signed with the same identifier and TeamID** (§10.1): TCC keys a grant to the client's code-signing identifier/designated requirement, so a `deckd` signed as `deckd` would get its own row and the hedge would degrade into re-prompt loops or silent denial. A denied grant yields silence, not an error (`host-research/voice-macos.md:100`), so `voice.route.permissions` and the Voice screen show "microphone denied" explicitly.

**What needs no grant at all, and what the dev loop cannot prove** (measured 2026-09-21). On-device STT and TTS need **no TCC grant**: `SFSpeechRecognizer` with `requiresOnDeviceRecognition = true` produced real partials and finals at `authorizationStatus == notDetermined`, from a terminal *and* from a launchd-spawned process with its own identity and no `Info.plist`, and `AVSpeechSynthesizer` needs nothing (V14). Only the microphone is gated, which is what makes week 4 testable against the real engines rather than only fakes. But a test that opens the mic from a terminal proves the **terminal's** grant and not ours (V9): TCC attributes the open to the responsible process, and for anything launched from a shell that is the terminal app. A launchd-spawned `deckd` is its own responsible process. So no test, no gate row and no line of this document may claim the daemon's grant path works until it has been run as the daemon — §16 Q5, week 7's gate (§10.4). Until then `perm.request` is built to its interface and **stubbed at the prompt**: it answers `{status:"prompt_not_shown"}` with the reason in `limits()`.

**On the BLE transport the deck needs Bluetooth, not Microphone.** Its microphone arrives as GATT notifications, so capturing it is a CoreBluetooth use, gated by the Bluetooth grant (System Settings → Privacy & Security → Bluetooth) and by `NSBluetoothAlwaysUsageDescription`: macOS terminates a process that touches CoreBluetooth without the string rather than denying it. The bundle's `Info.plist` carries it (§10.1), and the bare dev binary `tools/install-host.sh` starts from launchd carries its own copy in `__TEXT,__info_plist`. The Microphone grant is now only about the Mac's own input — `fallback_input = "system_default"` while the deck's link is down, and `voice.listen{input:"mac"}`. The grant is reported like the others: the link reads `CBManager.authorization` whenever the radio changes state, `deckd-ble-link` tells the core when it moves (`VoiceIn::Bluetooth`), and it is `permissions.bluetooth` in the snapshot and in `voice.test`, `bluetooth` in `perm.status` and in `perm.changed` (`unknown` on the CoreAudio transport and until the link starts). There is no `perm.request` for it: macOS asks by itself the first time the link starts, and a denial is lifted in System Settings. `deckd ble scan` prints it too, and a refusal also shows as the link's problem string ("this process may not use Bluetooth …"). V9's rule applies unchanged: a probe run from a terminal proves the terminal's Bluetooth grant, not the daemon's.

### 7.9 The BLE transport (`[voice] transport = "ble"`, the default)

`audio::spawn` builds `deckd-audio` around three things instead of CoreAudio devices: the link (`deckd::ble::BleAudio`), the deck's microphone as a `CaptureSource` (`audio::ble::BleCapture`) and its speaker as an `OutputSink` (`audio::ble::BleOutput`). No CoreAudio discovery and no property watcher run; `deckd-ble-link` turns link states into §7.2's route events. The key is read once, when the engine is built: `config.reload` and `config.set` report a changed `voice.transport` under `restart_needed`, while the rest of `[voice]` stays hot.

**Operator setup.** Pair the current deck in System Settings → Bluetooth, keep `[voice] transport = "ble"`, and grant Bluetooth to OpenWalkieTalkie (§7.8). The deck has no macOS Sound input/output entry with this firmware. A rebuilt binary must be installed and the running app/service restarted before it takes effect; changing the transport also needs a service restart. `deckd ctl --scopes observe voice.get` reports the active `transport` and route, and `deckd ctl --scopes voice voice.test '{"what":"speaker"}'` tests the custom BLE output. The standalone service can be rebuilt, installed and restarted with `tools/install-host.sh`; for a bundled app, replace the bundle, reopen it and use Service → Restart.

`config.get` includes the configured `voice.transport`; `voice.get` reports the running engine's transport. `voice.test` includes `mic_warm_effective`, which is `"never"` on BLE even when the configured `mic_warm` retains a Classic value.

**The link.** CoreBluetooth on a private serial queue (`deckd-ble`), never on the main run loop the speech engines own (V13). It looks for the deck among the peripherals **the system already holds connected** — by the audio service, then by the Battery service and the name `Codex Micro` — connects (for a deck the system holds for HID that is only our claim on a link that exists), discovers the service, reads the info characteristic and subscribes to the microphone, with a 10 s allowance per step; a failed attach is retried after 2 s, and each further failure in a row doubles the wait up to 60 s (a paired deck on firmware without the audio service is not connected, rediscovered and released every 2 s for as long as the daemon runs), until an attach succeeds, the radio comes back or the deck leaves the connected list. **The daemon never scans and never connects to an advertising deck**: connecting to an unpaired deck brings in macOS's HID service, which starts pairing and puts a pairing request on screen (`docs/deck-protocol.md`, "Pairing"), and a daemon must not do that on its own every two seconds — least of all to a neighbour's deck with the same name. A paired deck is one the system keeps connected for HID, which is exactly what the lookup finds; the bench probe below is the one link that scans, on purpose. Measured on the r6 bench, 2026-10-04: write limit 244 B (a full 166-byte frame fits), info `01 01 10 14 03` (version 1, IMA ADPCM, 16 kHz, 20 ms, mic + speaker); the first attach right after the system connected the deck sat in service discovery for over 10 s and the retry was ready in 6.6 s, which is why the timeout is per step; a 1 s MIC capture delivered 46 frames in 0.92 s (49.9 frames/s, 66.2 kbit/s), none lost, the first 120 ms after the request.

**The microphone** (`BleCapture`). The deck powers its PDM microphone and starts a stream on the press itself, and `deckd-audio` opens the capture when the press's channel-5 `ptt` line arrives over HID — which can be before or after the first audio notification. So a `deckd-ble-mic` thread reads the stream all the time and keeps a **pre-roll** of the current one from its START, at most 2 s (the last 2 s of a longer one); an open hands over the pre-roll and then the live frames, and an open with no stream live waits for the next START. A lost frame becomes silence of that frame's own length (the `seq` gap, and the tail an END reports), capped at 1 s per gap so a stall cannot push live audio out of the ring; a capture is one press, so once its stream ENDs it delivers nothing more, while a stream the link cut short (`END_ERROR`, also synthesised by the host when the link drops) sends it back to waiting, and so does the end of the very stream the previous capture was closed on (a quick re-press whose HID line beat the old stream's END over the radio: its own START follows, and is heard); a live stream silent for over a second (its END went missing) is not replayed into the next press. Samples reach the engine's sink at 16 kHz as mono f32 (`i16 / 32768`) from the reader's thread, as cpal's callback does from CoreAudio's, so `set mic`, the meter and V2's liveness bit are the same closure on both transports and the gain is applied once. The capture ring grew from 2 s to 4 s for the pre-roll: up to 2 s arrive in one go at the open, and a ring no bigger would drop the live frames behind it. With the link not ready (or a deck without a microphone) the open falls back as §7.1 does without the deck: `fallback_input = "system_default"` opens the Mac's input, `"none"` is `no_device`.

**The speaker** (`BleOutput`). There is no device clock to call us back, so `deckd-ble-out` owns the `Mixer` the way a cpal render closure does and renders it in 20 ms blocks on a monotonic clock, opened at 16 000 so the mixer does the 44.1 kHz → 16 kHz conversion (§7.2's V3 bridge), downmixed to mono `i16`. It runs **60 ms ahead** of real time (the probe's lead: the first 60 ms at once, then a block every 20 ms; "a lead of up to about 100 ms is fine" on the deck) and restarts its clock rather than bursting a backlog if it falls more than 100 ms behind. It frames **talk spurts**: START when a block holds a sample above 8 LSB (about −72 dBFS — with nothing playing the mixer rounds to exact zeros), the silent block before it first so a slow onset is never cut, AUDIO frames with one IMA coder state running across the spurt (each frame's header carries it, so any frame decodes alone), and END `release` after **300 ms** of silence — pauses between words and most between sentences stay inside one spurt, so the deck's amplifier (115 ms to power up, then an 80 ms DMA ring at 48 kHz: about 0.2 s from the first frame to sound) is not cycled mid-reply, and is off between announcements. It never waits for the radio: a frame the 64-frame outbox refuses is counted and raised as `Fault::Xrun` (the deck sees a `seq` gap and covers it with silence), a link that goes away cuts the spurt, and a close for our own capture sends END `abort` (after a link drop there is nothing to send it on, and the deck ends the stream itself: 300 ms with nothing, or the disconnect), joins the thread and gives the lanes back before the route machine asks for the next mixer. `voice.test{what:"speaker"}` exercises this whole path: the `ready` chime leaves through the speaker characteristic as one spurt, and the reply says `transport: "ble"`.

**An API capture asks for the microphone.** The deck streams on its own press, or when asked: `AudioCmd::Listen` carries `from_deck` (true only for the deck's own edge, `principal:"device"` with no session), and for anything else — `voice.listen{input:"deck"}` — `BleCapture` sends MIC mode 1 for the session's `max_s` (1 to 60) at the open and MIC mode 0 at the close. A press during such a capture takes the stream over on the deck: its release ends the stream, and the capture's MIC 0 no longer can, so an API listen closing never cuts off an operator who has just pressed.

**Not done yet.** Only the microphone has been heard on hardware; the speaker and loopback paths are tested against `FakeDeck` (frames, pacing, the chime end to end through the engine) and not yet on the r6.

**The bench** (no daemon needed). `deckd ble scan [--seconds N] [--name NAME]` only looks — the radio, the connected peripherals, the adverts — and never connects, so it is safe next to a deck in any state; exit 0 when the deck was seen. `deckd ble probe [--seconds N] [--tone HZ [--loopback]] [--name NAME]` asks one question each: no `--tone` captures with MIC mode 1 and reports frames, loss, level and the time to the first frame; `--tone` plays a −20 dBFS sine paced like the speaker above; `--loopback` keeps the speaker on during a MIC mode 2 capture and listens for the tone through the deck's own microphone. Exit 0 pass, 1 fail, 2 not ready within 20 s with the reason on the last line. Its link **does** scan, so on an unpaired deck it brings up the pairing request; stop the daemon before an active probe and restart it afterwards, so the probe and the service do not control the same microphone or speaker.

## 8. GUI — `OpenWalkieTalkie.app` (gpui 0.2.2 + gpui-component 0.5.1)


### 8.1 Architecture: the openmicrokbd pattern with the state as a mirror

Copied from openmicrokbd (`host-research/openmicrokbd.md:8-9,19-28,51`): `gpui_app::run()` bootstrap (`Application::new().with_assets`, `gpui_component::init`, `cmd-q`, `set_menus`, `open_window` (880×560 min 780×480 there; this app opens at 1040×780 with 1040×600 as its floor, §8.3), `app_id "ai.cohdl.openwalkietalkie"`, hide-on-close, `on_reopen`), `events.rs` (`async_channel::unbounded` bus, `events::post(AppEvent)`), `host_state.rs` (`HostState::handle_event(AppEvent) -> Vec<HostEffect>`, `HostState::detached()` for hermetic tests), `pixel.rs`/`gpui_controls.rs` theme, `menubar.rs` tray, dock/hide handling, `on_app_quit`, `i18n.rs`, `macos_updater.rs` Sparkle bridge, `auto-launch`.

What changes: `HostState::new()` starts no HID/audio/hotkey services. It starts an `ApiClient` (a `deck-client` connection) that connects to the **UDS only** (an unsandboxed app always has it; there is no WS fallback and no token on disk), sends `session.hello{client:{name:"OpenWalkieTalkie"}, scopes:["observe:content","operate","voice","device","admin"], subscribe:["*","transcript.partial","voice.level","device.key","device.macro","op.confirm_requested"]}`, learns from the reply whether it was verified as the trusted principal (`principal.trusted:true`, §4.2; `false` on an ad-hoc dev build without `[trust] dev_requirement`, shown as a red badge), and posts `AppEvent::Api(ApiEvent::{Connected{snapshot}, Snapshot{snapshot}, Event(Event), Disconnected, Resync})` onto the bus (`Snapshot` is a `state.get` reply: it replaces the mirror and closes the resync, and claims nothing about the socket — a reply that lands after the drop must not un-grey the window). The reducer folds them into a mirrored `Snapshot` (rev-checked; a gap triggers `state.get`) and returns `HostEffect::{ShowWindow, RaisePermissionSheet(kind), Toast(msg), Reconnect(after), OpenUrl, StateGet}` (`StateGet` because the reducer is pure: asking for a snapshot after a rev gap is something it can only *request*, never do). UI actions call `ApiClient::call(method, params)` futures and never mutate the mirror; the resulting events do.

**As built for the companion app (2026-11-11).** The hello asks for `voice`, `device` and `admin` only and says `"content": false` (`deck_gui_core::api_client`): the window shows counts and times and never a session (owner decision 1), so it asks for neither `observe:content` nor `operate`, and the service sends it no session text at all; an older service ignores the field, and the mirror drops the text instead. The subscription is the one above, unchanged, because every kind with a producer must reach the mirror for a jump in `rev` to keep meaning a hole. `HostEffect` is `{RaisePermissionSheet(kind), Toast(msg), Reconnect(after), StateGet}`. While no service answers, the frame's strip and the Deck page say which of three it is, as macOS records the launch agent: not running (**Start**, **Open Log**), never set up (**Set Up…**, first run's), or held by macOS until the owner allows it under Login Items (**Open Settings**).

**The tokio bridge, spelled out** (openmicrokbd has no tokio at all — its IPC is std threads + `events::post` — and Zed's `gpui_tokio` is a Zed-workspace crate against Zed-main gpui, not on crates.io and not buildable against `gpui =0.2.2`, `host-research/rust-service-stack.md:101`): at startup a std thread runs `tokio::runtime::Builder::new_multi_thread().worker_threads(2).enable_all().build()` and blocks on a shutdown future; its `Handle` is stored in a gpui `Global` (`struct Tokio(Handle)`); `Handle::spawn` runs the `deck-client` connection task (`UnixStream` + line codec + reconnect loop). Daemon → GUI: the connection task calls `events::post(AppEvent::Api(..))` — `async_channel::unbounded`'s `try_send`, executor-agnostic, exactly what openmicrokbd's HID thread does today. GUI → daemon: `ApiClient::call(method, params)` pushes `(Request, oneshot::Sender<Result<Value>>)` onto a `tokio::sync::mpsc` the connection task drains, and returns the `oneshot::Receiver`, which a view awaits inside `cx.spawn(async move |this, cx| { let r = rx.await; … })` — a plain `Future` runs fine on gpui's foreground executor, and the connection task on tokio does the I/O. Nothing ever calls `block_on` on the gpui thread; the `Handle` is only used for `spawn`. Reconnect: exponential back-off 250 ms → 5 s; while disconnected the window shows a yellow bar "Service not running" with Start (SMAppService) and "Open launchd log", the last mirror greyed. `HostState::detached()` keeps the 20-test reducer suite pattern with scripted `ApiEvent`s.

**Why the subscribe list names kinds one by one** (measured 2026-09-22). `deckd::bus::type_matches`
keeps high-volume kinds out of a **prefix glob as well as out of `"*"`**, so `"transcript.*"` does
**not** match `transcript.partial` — §4.5's rule is that high-volume kinds must be *named*, and the
matcher enforces it for globs too. The list therefore names `transcript.partial` exactly, or the Deck
screen's live dictation never populates. `device.key` and `device.macro` are named for a second
reason: `Store::emit` bumps `rev` for **every** event, including the high-volume ones `"*"` filters
out, so an un-named kind that has a producer burns revs the GUI never sees, the reducer correctly
calls the hole a hole, and the GUI pays a `state.get` per deck key press. Of the six high-volume
kinds, only `device.key`, `device.macro`, `transcript.partial` and `voice.level` have producers in
v1 — `device.raw` and `hook.raw` have none (both are deferred), and they would need `observe:raw`,
which the GUI does not hold. The proper fix, when a seventh kind arrives or `device.raw` lands, is a
contiguous per-subscription sequence beside `rev`; until then the named list is exact and a test
asserts every producing high-volume kind is covered.

**The build pin, measured 2026-09-22.** `gpui = "=0.2.2"` with **default features does not build here**: its build script shells out to Xcode's `metal` compiler and Xcode 27's Metal Toolchain is a separately downloadable component that is not installed (`xcodebuild -showComponent MetalToolchain` → `Status: uninstalled`), so the build dies after ~504 crates with `cannot execute tool 'metal'`. The pin is therefore

```toml
gpui = { version = "=0.2.2", default-features = false, features = ["runtime_shaders", "macos-blade"] }
gpui-component = "=0.5.1"
```

which compiles in 17.5 s and **opens and renders a real window** — `runtime_shaders` moves shader compilation past build time, so no Xcode component download is needed. `gpui-component` must **not** be "upgraded": 0.6.0 wants `gpui ^0.3.1`, 0.6.4 `^0.3.5` and 0.6.6 `=0.3.6`, and **no `gpui` 0.3.x is published** (crates.io tops out at 0.2.2), so the whole 0.6 line is unresolvable from the registry. 0.2.2 + 0.5.1 is the only registry-buildable pair.

**What the pin costs the rest of the workspace** (measured 2026-09-22). Adding gpui takes `host/Cargo.lock`
from 288 packages to 872, and it **moves one shared dependency down**: `gpui 0.2.2` pins
`core-foundation = "=0.10.0"`, `security-framework 3.7.0` accepts `^0.10`, so cargo unifies the whole
workspace onto 0.10.0 where it had been on 0.10.1. That matters more than a version bump usually
would, because `security-framework` is the **trust path** — `SecCode`, the requirement string and the
signature check of §4.2 all run through it. The whole suite passes on 0.10.0 and nothing else changed
version, but this is the one place a GUI dependency reaches into a security-critical one, so a future
gpui bump means re-reading the trust tests rather than trusting a green gate. It is also the argument
for `openwalkietalkie-app` carrying no logic: the crate that drags in 584 packages should not be the
crate that decides who is trusted.

**The tray's pin, measured 2026-09-22.** `tray-icon` must be **`=0.25.1`**, not the `0.19` this
document named. 0.19 wants `objc2 ^0.5.2` and `objc2-app-kit`/`objc2-foundation` `^0.2.2`, while
this workspace pins `objc2 =0.6.4` and the `objc2-*` family at `=0.3.2` for the voice and desktop
stacks — so resolving with 0.19 brings **five objc2 crates in at two major versions each**
(`objc2`, `objc2-app-kit`, `objc2-foundation`, `objc2-metal`, `objc2-quartz-core`), which is two
Objective-C binding layers in one process. 0.25.1 (with muda 0.20) resolves to a single version of
every one, matching the existing pins exactly, and was built and run: the status item appears and
the process lives under gpui's run loop. openmicrokbd runs 0.19 beside `gpui =0.2.2` without
trouble, but it has no audio stack and therefore never pins objc2 0.6 — that precedent does not
carry here.

**The login item's one trap.** `SMAppService.mainApp` works for an unbundled binary, which is
convenient and misleading: measured on a bare binary, `status` went 0 → `register()` **Ok** →
`status` **1 (enabled)** → `unregister()` → 0, but in a fresh state the first `register()` returned
**Ok while the status was 3 (`notFound`)**. The return value is therefore not evidence of anything.
The implementation registers, then **re-reads `status()`**, and reports success only on `enabled`
(1) or `requiresApproval` (2) — otherwise the toggle would say "on" having registered nothing.

**Two crates, so that most of this is testable without a GPU.** `deck-gui-core` carries the substance and depends on **no gpui at all**: the `async_channel` bus, the tokio bridge, `ApiClient`, `HostState`/`HostEffect` and the mirror reducer, with `HostState::detached()` and the hermetic reducer suite. `deck-gui` is the thin gpui layer — bootstrap, screens, tray. The gate runs `deck-gui-core`'s tests always and builds `deck-gui` behind a flag, so a machine without the Metal toolchain still gets a green gate on everything but the window.

### 8.2 Pages (the deck's companion, 2026-11-11)

*Rewritten for the companion app (PR #7, 2026-11-11). Until then this section designed an agent console: a roster mirror with a detail pane, card answers, an order box and hold-to-talk, a workflow viewer, session images, a Usage page, the confirmation sheet, Device, Settings and About screens, a 3-step Welcome sheet and a tray with a row per agent. All of that is gone from the window, and the service feeds the deck exactly as before. The window shows the deck, and the integrations as counts and times, never a session (owner decision 1).*

The sidebar has five pages, Deck first, and Service has two sub-pages. A page shows the mirror and the replies it keeps (a call's reply never folds into the mirror); every control goes through the service — `device.*`, `config.set`, `integrations.*`, `care.*` — and redraws from what comes back.

| Page | Content | Calls |
|---|---|---|
| **Deck** | The deck in the owner's hand: its picture showing the face it shows, in the case colour the owner picked; its link and transport, battery and charging, firmware and board; then **Needs attention**; then the deck's own settings in its panels' names and order (Volume and speech, Alerts, Display, Language). With no deck linked: when it was last seen, what to try, and the one setting that is still the owner's. With no service: why, as macOS records the launch agent (not running, never set up, or held for the owner's approval), with what can be done about it | `device.set`; the `device.status` event (§6.6) carries the battery, charging, transport and board |
| **Faces & Keys** | The deck's face strip as the deck last reported it (its pinned ends; move, add, remove), and the selected face's panel: a Macropad's nine keys (label, the deck control that presses it, the shortcut it types; **Record**), the Usage face's sources (Claude off until it is switched on), the Agents face's demo sessions, the Arcade face's sound | `device.faces.set`, `device.macro.set`, `config.set usage.providers`, `perm.status`/`perm.request` (Accessibility) |
| **Integrations** | What puts sessions on the deck — Claude Code, the Claude Channels bridge, Codex, Codex Desktop and the connected apps — as counts and last-event times, never a session; a switch per source and **Pause integrations**; **Set Up** and **Repair** through a plan dialog that shows the dry run before anything is written; **Send a Test Question**; Claude Channels' **How to Connect…** | `sources.list`, `integrations.status/install/repair/uninstall`, `config.set sources.*`/`codex.daemon`, `care.test.question` |
| **Voice** | The language the recognisers listen for; the two recognisers (Apple's, and the opt-in Whisper model) and the switch that lets Conol hear the presses meant for its chats; the voices (Apple's, a cloud voice with the owner's key, the opt-in Kokoro voice); the **Signal path** a press and a reply travel; a test from the Mac; the permissions this Mac grants the deck's voice | `config.set voice.*`, `stt.*`, `tts.*`, `voice.engines`, `care.test.*`, `perm.status`/`perm.request` |
| **Service** | Whether the service runs, with **Restart** and **Stop…**; **Start at login**; the two updates (this app, the deck's firmware); the Local API switch; **Check my setup** with **Export Support File** beside it; the line to Footprint | `service.info`, `service.restart`/`service.stop`, `firmware.*`, `config.set api.tcp`, `care.checks`, `care.support_file` |
| **Footprint** (under Service) | Everything the app has put on this Mac, a row each: where it is, its size, and what Uninstall does with it (**Remove…** for what the installer wrote, **Delete…** for the service's caches); **Uninstall…** takes it all back out (`deckd uninstall --purge --yes`, then the launch agent and the login item, which are the app's own) | `care.footprint`, `care.cache.clear`, `integrations.uninstall` |
| **Local API** (under Service) | The HTTP door's port, the browser origins it lets in, a `curl` to try it with, and the bearer tokens that open it (a plaintext shown once). The door is off by default, kept on only while an old install's Claude HTTP hooks still need it, and an explicit `[api] tcp` always wins; its removal is planned for the next release (§4.1) | `config.set api.*`, `token.*` |

**First run** (`firstrun.rs`) takes the whole window, with no sidebar, in four steps. **1 Connect** looks for the deck until it is plugged in or paired (`deckd probe --json` beside the app, every 2 s; it lists HID and opens nothing). **2 What this adds** names the background service and the Claude Code plugin and adds nothing until the owner presses **Add These**, which itself waits until a deck has been seen (owner decision 7); it then registers the launch agent, waits up to 20 s for the service to answer, has it add the plugin and reads the plugin's status back, and a step the service could not finish shows the one command that does, with **Copy Command**. **3 Voice** and **4 Integrations** show those pages' own cards, with **Continue**, **Send a Test Question** and **Finish**. **Not Now** adds nothing and only remembers that first run was seen; the Deck page then offers **Set Up…** for as long as the service is not set up.

**The status item** (`menu.rs`) is the sidebar's deck glyph in three states told apart by shape (linked; not linked, a slash across the screen; needs care, a dot) and a native menu: a status line, **Show Link Problem…** while the link has one, **Auto-Announce**, **Pause Integrations**, **Open OpenWalkieTalkie**, **Check for Updates…** and **Quit OpenWalkieTalkie**. The two toggles tick from what the service last reported, never from the click. There is no agent indicator and no row per agent. Closing the window hides the app: the service keeps running, the deck keeps working, and the status item opens the window again.

*The console's screen table, kept as the record of what was designed and built until 2026-11-11 (none of it is in the window now):*

| Screen | Content | Actions (API) |
|---|---|---|
| **Deck** (home) | Roster mirror grouped by project (the session's working directory, its last component as the header, the whole path in its tooltip; "No project" last — the same grouping the deck pages through with LEFT/RIGHT): state dot, kind icon, name (the kind alone when the name is the project's own), slot badge (or "not on deck"), live title (blank when it is just the project's name), state age, active-channel bar, owner/source badge (`hook`/`mcp`/`self-reported`/`script`); overflow rows sit in their project, marked "not on deck"; detail pane: full card text, all choices (paged cards flat), `#17.1/2`, hold countdown / delivery route / `failed` reason / `degraded` with "focused at", live workflow (plan steps, completed count, expandable recent tools), reply text, source (cwd, pid, terminal), caps, order history (the exact string typed or pushed, shown before an `inject` executes), `custom`-origin badge | `op.answer`, `op.snooze`, `op.ack`, `op.focus`, pin slot (`config.set agents.*`), text box → `op.order`, hold-to-talk button + `space` → `voice.listen{input:"mac"}`, Retry / Release to terminal on `failed`, Copy on `order.failed`; **confirmation sheet** on `op.confirm_requested` (an untrusted principal asked to allow a permission or inject; optional Touch ID) |
| **Device** | Link/session state, fw, sid, boot, battery when reported, pace, outbox depth, err counters; strip editor mirroring `faces` (drag to reorder, add/remove within the device's rules; refreshed from the next device `faces` event); settings mirror (all eight `set` keys read-write, persisted and restored on `boot:1`); Macropad editor: one row per Macropad instance in the strip (`f`, its spoken name) with a layout picker bound to `[macropad."<name>"]` (persisted in `state.json.macropad_bindings`) and a key editor that writes the layout; Usage feeders | `device.faces.set`, `device.set`, `device.macro.set`, `device.usage.set`, `device.raw` behind ⌥, "Pair in System Settings" (`x-apple.systempreferences:com.apple.BluetoothSettings`) |
| **Voice** | BLE transport by default: route diagram (BLE audio / our PTT / reopening / unavailable / Mac fallback), BLE endpoint identifiers, mic meter, STT engine/locale/on-device badge, TTS engine, per-agent voice picker with preview, announce/chime toggles. Bluetooth status links to Privacy & Security → Bluetooth; Microphone permission applies to Mac input/fallback; Speech authorization follows the selected STT engine (§7.8). Legacy `coreaudio` shows A2DP/SCO routes, CoreAudio UIDs and SCO timings. Transport changes require a service restart; Test reports the effective mic policy. | `voice.test`, `voice.say`, `config.set voice.*`, `perm.request` for Microphone/Speech; Bluetooth settings link |
| **Integrations** | One card per party (Claude Code, Codex CLI, Gemini CLI, Claude Desktop stdio entry, Ghostty Automation, third-party tokens): detected binary/version, status vocabulary (§5.7) incl. `hooks_hijack_risk` with the foreign listener's pid/command, "What this writes" diff (incl. the `allowedHttpHookUrls` note), Install / Update / Remove / Repair / **Pause hooks now**, backups with Restore, last event time, hold policy, "copy `deck-operator` prompt"; token list with scopes (plaintext shown once at creation, never again), create/revoke/rotate | `integrations.*`, `token.*`, `perm.request automation` |
| **Service** | SMAppService status with Start/Stop/Restart, port (with the degraded banner and "change port + re-install hooks"), allowed origins, `curl` example, live log tail with level filter and content-redaction toggle, `device.raw` view, Run diagnostics, Export bundle, Reveal logs | `service.*`, `config.set api.*`, `log.tail`, `diag.bundle` |
| **Settings** | Launch GUI at login via `SMAppService.mainApp.register()` (macOS 13+ is already the floor; `auto-launch`'s default AppleScript-to-System-Events path would raise an Automation TCC prompt for the GUI and add a second Login Items row beside the agent), dock icon policy, theme, language, update channel, Check for updates (Sparkle), Uninstall… ("uninstall before trashing the app") | GUI prefs plist; `deckd uninstall` |
| **About** | Versions (app, daemon, firmware, API), licences, Welcome again | |
| **Welcome sheet** (first run, re-openable) | v1, 3 steps: 1 Start service (SMAppService register, status, Login Items link) · 2 Connect the deck and voice (waits for `device.link`, shows fw + strip, "Not seeing it?" → Bluetooth settings + `doctor` summary; custom BLE audio is the default and the deck has no macOS Sound endpoint. Shows Bluetooth status/settings, Speech authorization for the selected STT engine, and Microphone grant for Mac input/fallback. Legacy Classic firmware can select `coreaudio`, with its own microphone grant. Test plays the ready chime through the selected transport and shows the route) · 3 Integrations (Install hooks per detected CLI; "hold PTT and say hello" live transcript as the finish line). v1.1 splits voice into its own step and adds the Claude Desktop entry and third-party token creation | as above |
| **Tray** | Template icon + aggregate dot (red question / green ready / blue working / grey none; **amber warning** on `service.degraded`/`hooks_hijack_risk`, with a one-time system notification "Port 47201 is held by <command>; Claude hooks may stall" that opens Integrations); one line per agent (`● cohdl-api · state · 2m`, no prompt text; click = focus + open card); Open; Mute announcements (`device.set announce`); Pause hooks (`config.set hooks.enabled=false`); Check for Updates…; Quit (GUI only — tooltip says the service keeps running); Stop service | |

*The Mac app’s **Usage** page went with the console (2026-11-11): the deck’s Usage face shows the bars, and the Faces & Keys page chooses their sources.* Claude’s model-specific weekly limits (including Fable) are read from `limits[].scope.model.display_name` even when common flat meters are also present. Status-line updates preserve unexpired model meters only for the same known account; account changes never inherit them. Fable precedes legacy model meters, with room reserved for Codex’s session/week meters within the device’s six-row limit.

*The Mac app’s Workflow viewer this paragraph describes went with the console (2026-11-11); plans and tool activity still reach the deck, as the paragraphs below say.* The Mac app’s Deck detail pane showed a live **Workflow** below any pending card. Explicit Codex `turn/plan/updated` events and Claude `TodoWrite` inputs supply plan steps; observed tools supply recent activity. The current step stays visible in long plans, with **Show all steps** for the full list. The latest four tools and any still running or expanded tools are shown by default; click a tool to reveal its input/output. Expansion survives updates and switching agents. Completed counts come only from explicit plan statuses, and disconnected sessions do not animate. Background workflow runs remain active when the parent chat is idle. Sessions without an explicit plan show tool activity without a guessed percentage.

`agent.reply.message` optionally carries `plan:{explanation?,steps:[{step,status}]}` (`status`: `pending`, `in_progress`, `completed`) and `activity` (`running`, `completed`, `failed`, `stopped`). Existing messages need neither field. `Agent.workflow` retains the latest plan after its message leaves the bounded chat tail, and a new user message clears it. Plans are limited to 64 steps, each 256 bytes, with a 1024-byte explanation. Plans and tool text follow the existing owner/`observe:content` rules; an `observe` snapshot or event reveals no plan text.

Claude’s `Workflow` runs appear first as compact rows on the deck (and, until 2026-11-11, in the Mac window): name, successful/started workers, progress bar, elapsed time, and tokens. The host recovers launches from the parent session and polls workflow journals plus worker usage every two seconds, including when started between hooks. It uses the latest input context (including cached input) plus deduplicated output tokens for each worker; in-flight token estimates only held in Claude’s memory are unavailable. Final snapshots provide authoritative terminal status and totals. Resumes reject old snapshots by task ID and exclude cached workers from earlier attempts. Finished rows remain for five minutes.

`agent.reply.message.runs` carries an optional list of `{id,name,status,completed,total,elapsed_ms,tokens}`. `Agent.workflow_runs` retains it across new parent messages and chat-tail eviction. Lists are bounded to 16 runs and follow the same content redaction as plans. Only public metadata and usage counters are retained; workflow scripts are never executed and worker prompts/results are not imported.

The physical deck has a **Workflow/Chat** toggle in each channel. Workflow uses the existing reading buffer and font sizes, showing a completed-count bar, three steps around the current step, and the latest three tools plus running or expanded tools. Tap disclosures for the full plan, older activity or syntax-highlighted tool output. ENTER switches views unless a pending card needs attention; CANCEL returns from Workflow to Chat; SHIFT+ENTER returns to the workflow's top. PTT and permission cards remain available. Firmware opts in through `doclines d:"w"`, so older firmware keeps receiving chat. Workflow refreshes retain the displayed page until its new revision arrives, with old disclosure targets disabled while pending. Workflow updates invalidate moved line offsets; the outbox keeps only the latest window and sends its matching revision envelope first. Both host and firmware must be updated.

The Codex event shape is documented in the [official app-server turn events](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/app-server#turn-events). Claude documents background runs in its [Dynamic Workflows guide](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/workflows).

The daemon works with the window closed: closing it hides the app, and the status item opens it again.

### 8.3 The look (redesigned 2026-11-11)

*Rewritten for the companion app. The 2026-10-04 look — Zeron's design language on a blurred, translucent window with one violet accent, and `OPENWALKIETALKIE_GLASS=0` to paint the shell opaque — is gone: the window is opaque, there is no window material and no blur code, and the `OPENWALKIETALKIE_GLASS` variable no longer exists.*

The look is taken from the deck's own screen (`style.rs`). Its values are the companion design's colour tokens (`DARK` and `LIGHT`), one `style::Palette` field each, and everything a page paints comes from the palette; `style::install` writes the same values into gpui-component's `Theme`, so its text fields, tooltips, notifications and menus match, and it runs again on every appearance change.

- **An opaque window** (`WindowBackgroundAppearance::Opaque`): the window is the palette's ground, never the desktop through glass. The title bar is transparent and has no title; the traffic lights sit on the sidebar's first row, and on first run's top bar.
- **Colour means state.** Nothing else in the window is coloured: the primary button, a switch that is on and a selected item are the neutral solid and outline, and keyboard focus is the one blue. A healthy state is a dot and a word, and only trouble gets a tinted pill. Light is designed, not inverted: its state colours step down to keep their contrast on white.
- **Type and icons.** Geist and Geist Mono from 11 to 30 px, registered from the binary (`assets/fonts`, OFL), with PingFang SC, PingFang TC and Hiragino Sans named for the Chinese and Japanese Geist does not have. Icons are Solar Linear (480 Design, CC BY 4.0) plus a few drawn to match; `assets/NOTICE` carries the attributions.
- **Components are the window's own** (`ui.rs`): the design's buttons, switch, level, segmented control, popup, radio, keycap, chip, status dot and pill, card, rows and tile, and in `art.rs` the deck itself (a picture per case colour and appearance), its face plates and its battery. Dialogs — the shell's own question, the Integrations plan, the token and uninstall dialogs — are drawn by the `Shell` from its own state.
- **The window** opens at the design's 1040×780 (a 212 px sidebar and a 764 px content column inside 32 px sides) and is never narrower than 1040 or shorter than 600.
- **Environment.** `OPENWALKIETALKIE_APPEARANCE=light|dark` pins the appearance; `OPENWALKIETALKIE_SCREEN` opens on another page than the Deck (how a run gate proves a page renders, since `runtime_shaders` compiles a shader only when something is drawn); `OPENWALKIETALKIE_FIRST_RUN=1` opens on first run whatever else holds, and `0` never does; `OPENWALKIETALKIE_PREFS=<file>` keeps the window's own preferences in that JSON file, as a window on a test service does.
- **`openwalkietalkie-app --shots DIR`** opens the real window, walks it through scenes, saves each as `DIR/<scene>-<dark|light>.png` (its own window, so no Screen Recording grant) and quits; one appearance per run, at the boards' 1040×780 unless `OPENWALKIETALKIE_SHOTS_SIZE` names another size. `OPENWALKIETALKIE_SHOTS_SYNTHETIC=1` draws the design's boards, and the states they do not draw, from fixtures, with no socket dialled; with `DECKD_DATA_DIR` set it walks the live scenes against the test service on that directory; with neither it draws nothing, because it would be drawing the installed service. `OPENWALKIETALKIE_SHOTS_ONLY` names the scenes, `OPENWALKIETALKIE_SHOTS_LANGUAGE` (`en`, `zh-Hans`, `zh-Hant`, `ja`) one language for every scene, and `OPENWALKIETALKIE_SHOTS_PREFLIGHT=1` first proves that the window is drawn at all.

Measured on gpui 0.2.2 / taffy 0.9 / macOS 27 while building it, and worth knowing before changing a layout:
